### Madame Bovary — Gustave Flaubert · `madame-bovary` · verified

**Old (unsourced):** France in 1857 saw Gustave Flaubert, the Revue de Paris’s director Léon Laurent‑Pichat, and the printer tried before the 6th criminal chamber of the Seine on charges of “outrage to public and religious morality and to good morals”; the court acquitted all defendants on 7 February 1857 but issued a formal blâme for the work’s “vulgar and often shocking realism” and the novel was then published intact in April 1857 with great popular success.

**New (sourced):** In 1857, Gustave Flaubert, the owner and the printer of La Revue de Paris were tried before the Tribunal correctionnel de la Seine over the serialised publication of Madame Bovary, accused of insulting public morals and offending decent manners; they were acquitted in February 1857.

  - [napoleon.org](https://www.napoleon.org/en/history-of-the-two-empires/close-up/a-close-up-on-the-flaubert-madame-bovary-trial) (other): "7 February, 1857, saw the end of the trial involving the writer Gustave Flaubert, the owner, and the printer of La Revue de Paris. ### Scary Stories to Tell in the Dark — Alvin Schwartz · `scary-stories-to-tell-in-the-dark` · verified

**Old (unsourced):** Challenge in 1990 occurred in Livonia, Michigan schools, where poems and stories were deemed frightening to children and formally challenged; other notable incidents include 1992 at Neely Elementary School in Gilbert, Arizona, for occult, devil and satanism themes, and multiple challenges across Connecticut, Indiana, Washington, Kentucky, and elsewhere, often citing violence, occult content and being unsuited to the age group; outcomes varied but many resulted in restricted access or removal from elementary school libraries.

### The Bell Jar — Sylvia Plath · `the-bell-jar` · verified

**Old (unsourced):** Banned by the Warsaw Community School Corporation (Warsaw, Indiana) in 1977, at the school‐level in a “Women in Literature” course, on grounds including profanity. The removal was reviewed by a Federal Court of Appeals, which upheld the school board’s decision, leaving the ban in place. Additional challenges occurred in Edwardsville, Illinois (1981) and Richland, Washington (1998), but those did not result in successful bans in classrooms.

**New (sourced):** In the late 1970s, The Bell Jar was removed from a high school “Women in Literature” course in Indiana after it was deemed unsuitable for high school students because of its profanity, sexuality and rejection of the woman’s role as wife and mother; the teacher was not rehired.

**Why:** 1 sentence(s) from 1 source(s)

Old claims: 4 · supported verbatim: 0 · partial: 1 · contradicted: 0 · pages read: 2 · quotes not on page: 0

  - [explore.lib.virginia.edu](https://explore.lib.virginia.edu/exhibits/show/censored/walkthrough/minorities) (institutional): "In the late 1970s, The Bell Jar was suppressed for not only its profanity and sexuality but for its overt rejection of the woman's role as wife and mother. For these reasons, the book was deemed unsuitable for high school students in Indiana. The Bell Jar ultimately was removed from a high school "Women in Literature" course, and the teacher was not rehired."

### My Secret Life — Walter · `my-secret-life` · verified

**Old (unsourced):** "My Secret Life" by Walter was banned in New Zealand in 1888 at the national level for obscenity, reflecting the strict moral standards of the time. The book faced further scrutiny in 1968 and 1976 when it was deemed indecent, although it was allowed for individuals over 18 engaged in relevant professional or academic studies. This historical context highlights the ongoing tension between literary expression and societal norms regarding sexuality during the Victorian era and beyond.

**New (sourced):** In 1968, the Indecent Publications Tribunal ruled My Secret Life indecent except in the hands of people over 18 whose professional or academic studies covered its subject. In 1976, the Indecent Publications Tribunal again ruled My Secret Life indecent except in the hands of people over 18 whose professional or academic studies covered its subject.

### Justine — Marquis de Sade · `justine-de-sade` · verified

**Old (unsourced):** In 1791, French authorities banned "Justine" by Marquis de Sade at the national level due to its sexual content and obscenity. In 1965, the New Zealand Indecent Publications Tribunal classified the novel as indecent under the Indecent Publications Act 1963, leading to its prohibition in the country. Additionally, in 1966, the U.S. District Court for the Southern District of New York ordered the forfeiture and destruction of copies of "Justine" imported from France, citing obscenity concerns. These actions reflect the historical suppression of Sade's works across various nations due to their controversial themes.

**New (sourced):** In 1791, authorities in France suppressed Justine or the Misfortunes of Virtue.

**Why:** 1 sentence(s) from 1 source(s)

### The Rainbow — D.H. Lawrence · `the-rainbow-dh-lawrence` · verified

**Old (unsourced):** In 1915, D.H. Lawrence's novel "The Rainbow" was prosecuted for obscenity in Britain, leading to the confiscation and destruction of 1,011 copies. This ban rendered the novel unavailable in Britain until 1926. The prosecution cited the book's candid treatment of sexuality, including a lesbian relationship, and its critique of industrial capitalism.

**New (sourced):** In November 1915, British authorities prosecuted The Rainbow’s publisher, Methuen, under the Obscene Publications Act of 1857 over the novel’s treatment of sexual love; after the trial, copies were seized and burnt, and the book was banned in Britain for 11 years.

### Azadi — Arundhati Roy · `azadi` · verified

**Old (unsourced):** In 2025, "Azadi: Freedom. Fascism. Fiction." by Arundhati Roy was banned at the national level in India, specifically in the union territory of Jammu and Kashmir, as part of a broader crackdown on approximately two dozen titles deemed to "propagate false narratives" or "promote secessionism." This ban followed the central government's reorganization of the region and faced significant backlash from Indian writers' organizations and PEN, highlighting concerns over freedom of expression and the treatment of dissenting voices in contemporary India. ### The Hunchback of Notre-Dame — Victor Hugo · `the-hunchback-of-notre-dame` · verified

**Old (unsourced):** "The Hunchback of Notre-Dame" was banned in Vatican City in 1834, primarily due to its portrayal of corrupt clergy and its anti-clerical themes, leading to its inclusion on the Church's Index of Forbidden Books, where it remained until 1966. This ban reflected the Church's concerns over the novel's moral implications and its challenge to religious authority during a time of significant social upheaval in Europe.

**New (sourced):** Rome condemned The Hunchback of Notre-Dame in 1834; it was listed on an index of books prohibited by the Roman Catholic church. The index listing of The Hunchback of Notre-Dame ended on 14 June 1966.

### Just Mercy — Bryan Stevenson · `just-mercy` · verified

### Naked Lunch — William S. Burroughs · `naked-lunch` · verified_additions

**Old (unsourced):** Massachusetts prosecuted Naked Lunch in Boston in a 1965 in rem obscenity trial; the Superior Court found it obscene, but the Massachusetts Supreme Judicial Court overturned the ban in Attorney General v. A Book Named “Naked Lunch” (1966), holding the work was not utterly without redeeming social value. Customs and postal authorities in the U.S. also embargoed the work under obscenity laws; no further named governmental or institutional bans appear beyond the national bans in the U.K. (1964) and New Zealand (ruled indecent in 1967 by the Indecent Publications Tribunal, with restrictions later relaxed).

**New (sourced):** In 1964, the Attorney General filed a petition concerning Naked Lunch in the Superior Court, which adjudged the book obscene; the final decree was later reversed, with a new decree to declare that the book could not be declared obscene. US Customs embargoed Naked Lunch as allegedly obscene under the Tariff Act (1930). In 1967, the Tribunal in New Zealand found Naked Lunch indecent; it later classified the book as indecent in the hands of persons under the age of 18.

**Why:** US Customs embargoed Naked Lunch as allegedly obscene under the Tariff Act (1930). · The New Zealand Tribunal later classified Naked Lunch as indecent in the hands of persons under 18.

### Eleanor & Park — Rainbow Rowell · `eleanor-and-park` · verified_additions

**Old (unsourced):** Yamhill‑Carlton School District in Oregon removed Eleanor & Park from an eighth‑grade classroom in January 2017 without following review policy, then reversed the decision that month by convening a reconsideration committee as required, allowing proper review. In July 2013, the Anoka‑Hennepin School District in Minnesota withdrew an invitation to Rainbow Rowell and faced a formal challenge calling the novel “vile profanity”; a review committee later unanimously voted to retain the book on school library shelves. Additional formal removals from district libraries and classrooms occurred in Indian River County, Florida, in November 2021, and Fredericksburg ISD, Texas, in March 2022.

**New (sourced):** The Yamhill-Carlton School District in Western Oregon removed Eleanor & Park in January after parents complained about profanity and passages they said encouraged “bad behavior”; the school board later agreed to convene a Reconsideration Committee, while the book stayed out of middle school classrooms and high school libraries in the interim. In 2013, parents of a high school freshman and the Parents Action League challenged Eleanor & Park in school libraries, citing profanity and sexuality; a committee later voted to retain it in Anoka-Hennepin Public Schools.

### Boy — James Hanley · `boy-james-hanley` · verified_additions

**Old (unsourced):** In 1934, James Hanley's novel "Boy" was prosecuted for obscenity in the United Kingdom. The case originated from a complaint in Bury, near Manchester, where the book's cover and content were deemed suggestive and potentially harmful to young minds. The publisher, Boriswood, was advised that any defense against prosecution was futile due to the book's references to 'intimacy between members of the male sex.' In March 1935, Boriswood pleaded guilty to 'uttering and publishing an obscene libel' and paid a substantial fine. This prosecution effectively ended the first edition of "Boy" and damaged Hanley's career for years. The novel was later republished by the Obelisk Press in Paris in 1935 and 1946.

**New (sourced):** A court case against Boy began in November 1934 and culminated in a trial in March 1935; the prosecuting solicitor, Mr. Jessel Ryecroft, suggested that its cover and review extracts were intended to pollute young people’s minds. Boriswood was forced to withdraw Boy from sale in January 1935.

**Why:** Boriswood was forced to withdraw Boy from sale in January 1935.

The novel’s publication history illustrates how censorship can delay, fragment, and reshape literary works over decades.

### Push — Sapphire · `push` · verified_additions

**Old (unsourced):** Alpine School District in Utah removed Push by Sapphire in 2022 under a new state law (HB 374, “Sensitive Materials In Schools”), citing pornographic content; the removal was upheld under district policy. Redlands Unified School District in California permanently removed Push from all high school libraries following a 3–2 board vote in December 2025, overriding prior recommendations to retain it with restrictions.

**New (sourced):** In August 2022, Push was pulled from Utah's Alpine School District. The Redlands school board voted 3–2 to remove Push from schools after reviewers recommended keeping it with restricted access and the original complainant appealed that decision.

**Why:** The Redlands school board’s 3–2 vote to remove Push from schools after reviewers recommended restricted access and the original complainant appealed.

### The Golden Compass — Philip Pullman · `the-golden-compass` · verified_additions

**Old (unsourced):** Halton District Catholic School Board in Ontario, Canada voted in December 2007 to ban The Golden Compass and the other two books in Philip Pullman’s His Dark Materials trilogy from school libraries, rejecting a review committee’s recommendation to reclassify the book to a young‑adult section instead of removal. Calgary Catholic School District in Alberta, Canada also quietly removed the book from school shelves in late 2007 pending review, although it was later returned. In the United States, a school‑level ban was recorded in 2007 for reasons of religious or blasphemous content, but no specific school district or court case could be identified.

**New (sourced):** In 2007, The Golden Compass was pulled from St. John Neumann Middle and Lourdes High School in Oshkosh, WI, over concerns about what critics called its “anti-Christian message.” In 2008, The Golden Compass was removed from library shelves at dozens of schools in the publicly funded Halton (Ontario, Canada) Catholic School District after a challenge alleging that it was “written by an atheist where the characters and text are anti-God, anti-Catholic, and anti-religion”; it was later returned. The Calgary (Alberta, Canada) Catholic School District ordered The Golden Compass removed from its library shelves after detractors accused it of anti-religious content, then returned it two months later.

**Why:** 2008 removal and later return at schools in the Halton (Ontario, Canada) Catholic School District. · Removal and return two months later in the Calgary (Alberta, Canada) Catholic School District. · ban in Canada 2008: no ban row for that country · lift in Canada: no ban row for that country · ban in

### It's Perfectly Normal — Robie Harris · `its-perfectly-normal` · verified_additions

**Old (unsourced):** In 2002, Montgomery County, Texas, commissioners ordered the immediate removal of 'It's Perfectly Normal' from county library shelves, citing complaints that the book was obscene and promoted homosexuality and abortion. In 2008, a patron in Lewiston, Maine, removed the book from the Lewiston and Auburn public libraries, claiming it was 'sufficiently horrified by the illustrations and sexually graphic, amoral, abnormal contents.' A police investigation found that the library did not violate the town ordinance against obscenity, and the patron faced theft charges. Additionally, in 2005, the book was the most frequently challenged book in the United States, according to the American Library Association.

**New (sourced):** At an Aug. 26 Commissioners Court meeting, commissioners ordered It's Perfectly Normal removed from county library shelves pending a content review after several residents complained that it was obscene.

Go Tell It on the Mountain was challenged and placed under review in sophomore-level Academic English II classes at Lemont (IL) High School District 210 in 2017 because a person described as “a patent” considered it “pornographic.”

**Old (unsourced):** Banned at the national level in Canada in 1949 under the Minister of National Revenue’s authority, along with ninety-six other titles, excluded from importation for obscenity and not adjudicated in a court; same year, banned in Australia in 1949, though no specific court case or institutional decision by name is documented, with the prohibition effected under national censorship laws due to language and sexual content.

### One Flew Over the Cuckoo's Nest — Ken Kesey · `one-flew-over-the-cuckoos-nest` · verified_additions

**Old (unsourced):** Strongsville, Ohio in 1974 saw five residents sue the Board of Education, seeking removal from classrooms on grounds of pornography, glorification of criminal behavior, and descriptions of bestiality, bizarre violence, torture, dismemberment, death, and human elimination. Randall, New York, and Alton, Oklahoma removed it from all public schools in 1975; Freemont High School in St. Anthony, Idaho banned it in 1978 and dismissed the teacher who assigned it. Merrimack High School in New Hampshire challenged it in 1982, and Aberdeen High School in Washington was challenged in 1986 but retained for Honors English.

**New (sourced):** In 1974, five residents of Strongsville, OH, sued the board of education to remove One Flew Over the Cuckoo's Nest, calling it pornographic and alleging that it glorified criminal activity, could corrupt juveniles, and depicted bestiality and violence. In 1975, One Flew Over the Cuckoo's Nest was removed from public school libraries in Randolph, NY. In 1975, One Flew Over the Cuckoo's Nest was removed from public school libraries in Alton, OK. In 1978, One Flew Over the Cuckoo's Nest was banned from St. Anthony, ID Freemont High School classrooms; the instructor was fired and sued, but the decision was never published. In 1982, One Flew Over the Cuckoo's Nest was challenged at Merrimack, NH High School. In 1986, One Flew Over the Cuckoo's Nest was challenged in an Aberdeen, WA High School honors English class on the grounds that it promoted “secular humanism”; the school board voted to retain it.

**Why:** 1974 Strongsville, Ohio, lawsuit seeking removal of the book · 1975 removal from public school libraries in Randolph, New York · 1975 removal from public school libraries in Alton, Oklahoma · 1978 ban from St. Anthony, Idaho, Freemont High School classrooms and related teacher lawsuit · 1982 challen

### Fight Club — Chuck Palahniuk · `fight-club` · verified_additions

**Old (unsourced):** Katy Independent School District in Texas removed *Fight Club* from its required reading list in 2013 following complaints about violent and explicit content; the decision was enacted at the district level and has not been publicly reversed. ### Jinnah of Pakistan — Stanley Wolpert · `jinnah-of-pakistan` · verified_additions

**Old (unsourced):** "Jinnah of Pakistan" by Stanley Wolpert was banned in Pakistan in 1984 under the regime of military dictator Zia-ul-Haq, who cited 'offending passages' as the reason for the ban. The prohibition was part of a broader crackdown on literature deemed contrary to the government's narrative, particularly concerning the founding figures of Pakistan. The ban was lifted in 1989 following the return to a democratic government, which sought to reverse many of Zia's policies.

In 1989, Benazir and her government gave OUP permission to reprint Jinnah of Pakistan, and the book was released for sale in Pakistan.

### Hit Man: A Technical Manual for Independent Contractors — Rex Feral · `hit-man-technical-manual` · verified_additions

**Old (unsourced):** Rice v. Paladin Enterprises, Inc. (Fourth Circuit, 1997) reversed a district court’s summary judgment and held that publication and distribution of the murder manual “Hit Man: A Technical Manual for Independent Contractors” could give rise to civil aiding‑and‑abetting liability because the publisher intended to assist criminal conduct. In a separate federal lawsuit filed in the District of Oregon in 1998, a victim similarly sued the publisher under product liability theories; that case was dismissed at first but ultimately settled out of court. Documentation also records a 1990 national‑level ban of the book in Australia on grounds of violence.

**New (sourced):** In 1996, the family of a Maryland murder victim sued the publisher of Hit Man: A Technical Manual for Independent Contractors in U.S. District Court; a federal district court judge dismissed the suit on First Amendment grounds, but the Fourth Circuit reversed, holding that the book’s content was criminal aiding and abetting and was not entitled to First Amendment protection. In 2001, victims of an attempted contract murder sued the publisher of Hit Man: A Technical Manual for Independent Contractors in an Oregon federal court for aiding and abetting and conspiracy to commit murder; the court allowed the claims to go forward.

**Why:** The 1996 Maryland murder victim’s family lawsuit and the Fourth Circuit reversal. · The 2001 Oregon federal lawsuit by victims of an attempted contract murder and the court’s decision to allow the claims to go forward.

### The Gulag Archipelago — Aleksandr Solzhenitsyn · `the-gulag-archipelago` · verified_additions

**Old (unsourced):** Aleksandr Solzhenitsyn’s The Gulag Archipelago was banned in the Soviet Union following its publication in the West in 1973. The work documented the Soviet forced labor camp system in detail, drawing on personal testimony and archival material. Solzhenitsyn was arrested and expelled from the Soviet Union in 1974. The book circulated clandestinely in samizdat form and was only officially published in Russia in 1989. This case is central to understanding censorship of political dissent during the Cold War.

**New (sourced):** In 1973, an archive containing the manuscript of The Gulag Archipelago was seized; its holder, Elizaveta Voronyanskaya, died by suicide, and the book was published in Paris that December. In February 1974, Soviet authorities retaliated against the publication of The Gulag Archipelago by arresting Aleksandr Solzhenitsyn, stripping him of his Soviet citizenship and sending him against his will to Frankfurt, West Germany.

**Why:** 1973 seizure of an archive containing the manuscript, Elizaveta Voronyanskaya’s suicide, and publication in Paris that December. · February 1974 arrest of Aleksandr Solzhenitsyn, revocation of his Soviet citizenship, and involuntary transfer to Frankfurt, West Germany.

### Ars Amatoria — Ovid · `ars-amatoria` · verified_additions

**Old (unsourced):** In 8 AD, Emperor Augustus exiled Ovid to Tomis on the Black Sea, reportedly due to the publication of "Ars Amatoria," a work perceived as subversive to his moral reforms. The poem was later included in the Vatican's Index of Forbidden Books, a list of publications prohibited by the Catholic Church. In 1497, during the Bonfire of the Vanities in Florence, Italy, Ovid's works were burned as immoral and corruptive. Additionally, in 1599, the Archbishop of Canterbury and the Bishop of London ordered the burning of Christopher Marlowe's translation of Ovid's "Elegies," among other works, as part of the Bishops' Ban.

**New (sourced):** In 1564, Ars Amatoria was added to the Catholic Index for “treating of lascivious or obscene objects.” In 1497, Ovid’s works, including Ars Amatoria, were burned in the Piazza della Signoria, Florence, at the instigation of Savonarola, an Italian Dominican priest who crusaded against “immoral art.”

**Why:** In 1564, Ars Amatoria was added to the Catholic Index for treating lascivious or obscene subjects. · In 1497, Ovid’s works, including Ars Amatoria, were burned in Florence at Savonarola’s instigation.

### Our Bodies, Ourselves — Boston Women's Health Book Collective · `our-bodies-ourselves` · verified_additions

**Old (unsourced):** In 1977, "Our Bodies, Ourselves" faced removal from school libraries in several states across the United States due to challenges citing immorality and sexual content. Notably, the book was contested in various school districts, leading to its removal from public library collections in states such as Texas and Florida. The challenges often stemmed from concerns over its candid discussions of sexuality, abortion, and lesbian relationships, resulting in the book appearing on the American Library Association's most-challenged lists in subsequent decades. Despite these challenges, "Our Bodies, Ourselves" has remained a significant work in feminist health literature, continuing to be published and translated worldwide.

### Boy Toy — Barry Lyga · `boy-toy` · verified_additions

**Old (unsourced):** Request to remove Boy Toy by Barry Lyga from Dover High School’s library in Dover, New Hampshire, occurred when resident Julie Porter filed a formal reconsideration request in 2023; school librarian and superintendent recommended retaining the book, and the Dover School Board unanimously voted to keep it on library shelves in November 2023. No other specific bans or legal cases regarding the book have been documented, aside from its removal from a Davis School District high school library in Utah effective December 18, 2023, under Utah Code § 76‑10‑1227 due to the determination that it contained sensitive material deemed to lack serious value for minors.

The Dover School Board unanimously voted to keep Boy Toy at the Alumni Drive school after taxpayer Julie Porter requested reconsideration.

### Black Boy — Richard Wright · `black-boy` · verified_additions

**Old (unsourced):** In 1972 Michigan schools removed Black Boy from classrooms at parents’ request citing sexual content and unsuitability for teens, with no identified court case or reinstatement. Pico (1982) ruled restrictions on library books cannot be enforced if based purely on officials’ personal views, effectively invalidating the removal. ### David Hume — All works — David Hume · `david-hume-all-works` · verified_additions

**Old (unsourced):** In 1761, the Vatican City (Holy See) officially banned David Hume's "Dialogues Concerning Natural Religion" at the government level, citing concerns over religious blasphemy. This ban reflected the Catholic Church's broader efforts to suppress works that questioned or critiqued traditional religious beliefs, particularly those that engaged with the nature of God and human understanding of the divine. The decision was part of a historical pattern of censorship aimed at maintaining doctrinal orthodoxy during a time of significant philosophical and theological debate in Europe.

**New (sourced):** David Hume’s books were banned starting in 1761 and added to the Index Librorum Prohibitorum, the official list of books Catholics were forbidden to publish or read. After Dialogues was published in 1779 and Hume was identified as its author, it was added to his other banned works.

**Why:** After Dialogues was published in 1779 and Hume was identified as its author, it was added to his other banned works.

Old claims: 1 · supported verbatim: 0 · partial: 1 · contradicted: 0 · pages read: 3 · quotes not on page: 1

- David Hume’s books were banned starting in 1761 and added to the Index Librorum Prohibitorum, the official list of books Catholics were forbidden to publish or read.
  - [oll.libertyfund.org](https://oll.libertyfund.org/publications/reading-room/2023-10-25-donway-hume-great-work-on-religion-banned) (other): "Dialogues was published in 1779, without Hume’s name as author. But when the author was identified (it did not take long), the book was added to other works by Hume that had been banned starting in 1761. Hume was one of those authors all of whose books were added to the Index Librorum Prohibitorum, the official list of books, actively maintained until 1948, that Catholics were forbidden to publish or to read."
- After Dialogues was published in 1779 and Hume was identified as its author, it was added to his other banned works.

**Old (unsourced):** In 1979, "A Minor Apocalypse" was banned in Poland at the national level due to its political content. The novel circulated in samizdat, an underground publishing movement, as it critiqued both the government and the opposition, highlighting the absurdities of life under totalitarianism. The ban was part of a broader crackdown on dissenting literature during a time when the regime sought to suppress any form of opposition leading up to the rise of the Solidarity movement in the early 1980s.

**New (sourced):** Tadeusz Konwicki claimed that NOW-a censored A Minor Apocalypse because of “certain resemblances” to key oppositionists; he agreed to the constraints.

**Old (unsourced):** Customs authorities in Canada banned Droll Stories (Les Contes drolatiques) at the national level in 1914 on grounds of obscenity; that ban was enforced for years. Australian Customs placed Droll Stories on its prohibited import list under the Commonwealth Customs Act from 1901, leading to prosecutions such as bookseller George Robertson being charged for possession, with the ban persisting in various forms until lifted. In New Zealand the Customs Department listed Droll Stories as a prohibited import under the Hicklin Rule, enforced via Magistrate’s Court until it was eventually removed.

**New (sourced):** In New Zealand, a Magistrate's Court banned Droll Stories in 1935; it was also on the Customs Department's list of prohibited books and was eventually removed. Magistrate Mr Wyvern Wilson found Droll Stories to be an indecent document and fined bookseller Cecil George Swallow £5 Is and costs for offering it for sale. The Commonwealth of Australia Department of Customs and Excise seized a 1928 hardcover edition of Droll Stories as a prohibited book.

### Les Onze Mille Verges — Guillaume Apollinaire · `les-onze-mille-verges` · record_conflict

**Old (unsourced):** In 1907, Guillaume Apollinaire's novel "Les Onze Mille Verges" was banned in France at the national level due to its explicit sexual content and perceived obscenity. The book remained censored in France until 1970. In 1999, Turkish publisher Rahmi Akdaş published a Turkish translation of the novel, leading to his conviction under Turkish law for publishing obscene material. The European Court of Human Rights ruled in 2010 that Turkey violated Akdaş's freedom of expression, stating that the censorship hindered public access to a work belonging to the European literary heritage. The court found the seizure and destruction of the book and the heavy fine imposed on Akdaş were not proportionate to the legitimate aim pursued and thus violated Article 10 of the European Convention on Human Rights.

**New (sourced):** In October 1999, prosecuting authorities in Turkey sought the conviction of the publisher of Les Onze Mille Verges for publishing obscene or immoral material liable to arouse and exploit sexual desire; in September 2000, a court fined him and ordered all copies seized and destroyed, but the Court of Cassation later quashed the destruction order and upheld the fine.

**Why:** text: The Court of Cassation later quashed the order to destroy copies and upheld the fine. ↔ records: The Turkey 2000 row note says the conviction led to the seizure and destruction of all copies.

### Inside Linda Lovelace — Linda Lovelace · `inside-linda-lovelace` · record_conflict

**Old (unsourced):** In 1976, the publishers of "Inside Linda Lovelace" were prosecuted under the UK's Obscene Publications Act 1959. The trial concluded with the jury acquitting the publishers, determining that the book did not meet the legal definition of obscenity. This outcome effectively ended prosecutions of written material under the Act, as authorities believed that if "Inside Linda Lovelace" was not obscene, then nothing was. Consequently, the Metropolitan Police ceased such prosecutions, and the law was rarely invoked against the written word thereafter.

**New (sourced):** In 1976, the publishers of Inside Linda Lovelace were prosecuted on an obscenity charge at the Old Bailey and were acquitted.

**Why:** text: The publishers were prosecuted for obscenity in 1976 and acquitted. ↔ records: The book was banned in the United Kingdom in 1976 under obscenity laws.

### The Blind Owl — Sadeq Hedayat · `the-blind-owl` · record_conflict

**Old (unsourced):** In 1936–37, publication in Iran was blocked under Reza Shah’s regime because of atheist and erotic passages, prompting Hedayat to produce a handwritten edition in Bombay labeled “Not for sale or distribution in Iran.” In 1941, censorship relaxed under Mohammad Reza Pahlavi and the work began circulating in Iran. Following the 1979 Islamic Revolution, authorities deemed it anti‑religious, banned republication, and reportedly tried the publisher; a heavily censored edition was allowed in 1993, with broader publication still restricted.

**New (sourced):** In 1936, Hedayat was told he could publish The Blind Owl only if he removed certain atheist passages; he refused and went to India, where the book was published. In 1941, Iran’s new shah, Mohammed Reza Pahlavi, removed all censorship restrictions, and The Blind Owl was brought to Iran. After Hedayat’s death, The Blind Owl was banned again. In 1979, with the establishment of the Islamic Republic, The Blind Owl was considered anti-religious, and its publisher was put on trial. In 1993, The Blind Owl was allowed to be published if heavily censored, with religious and sexual passages cut.

**Why:** text: The ban ended in 1993 when publication was allowed if the book was heavily censored. ↔ records: The 1979 Iran ban is listed as active.

**New (sourced):** San Francisco customs inspectors seized part of the second printing of Howl in March 1957. Ferlinghetti was arrested on obscenity charges arising from the publication of Howl in June 1957; the American Civil Liberties Union bailed him out and led his defense, and he was found not guilty. In the resulting obscenity trial over Howl, prosecutors could not prove that co-defendant Murao knew the book’s contents, and he was cleared; the case concluded on October 3, 1957, when Judge Clayton W. Horn ruled that the poem had “redeeming social importance.”
